&#x20;

### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Pakistan)

Since independence, Pakistan has aimed to maintain an independent foreign policy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELodhi2022-292] Pakistan's [foreign policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_policy_of_Pakistan) and [geostrategy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_geostrategy) focus on the economy, security, [national identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalism_in_Pakistan), and territorial integrity, as well as building close ties with other Muslim nations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamid_et_al.2023-293] According to foreign policy expert [Hasan Askari Rizvi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasan_Askari_Rizvi), "Pakistan highlights sovereign equality of states, bilateralism, mutuality of interests, and non-interference in each other's domestic affairs as the cardinal features of its foreign policy."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERizvi2004-294]&#x20;



President [Ayub Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayub_Khan) meeting with U.S. President [John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy) in 1961

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPicone2020-295]

The [Kashmir conflict](3030955.md) remains a major issue between Pakistan and India, with three of their [four wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_wars_and_conflicts) fought over it.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart-IngersollFrazier2012-296] Due partly to strained relations with India, Pakistan has close ties with Turkey and Iran, both focal points in its foreign policy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnwar2006-297] Saudi Arabia also holds importance in Pakistan's foreign relations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPande2011167-298]&#x20;

As a non-signatory of the [Treaty on Nuclear Non-Proliferation](22107.md), Pakistan holds influence in the [IAEA](14984.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChakma2012-299] For years, Pakistan has blocked an [international treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fissile_Material_Cut-off_Treaty) to limit [fissile material](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fissile_material), arguing that its stockpile does not meet its long-term needs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKmentt2021-300] Pakistan's nuclear program in the 20th century aimed to counter India's nuclear ambitions in the [region](21566765.md), and [reciprocal nuclear tests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan%27s_nuclear_testing_series) ensued after India's [nuclear tests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pokhran-II), solidifying Pakistan as a [nuclear power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_power_in_Pakistan).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIzuyamaOgawa2003-301] Pakistan maintains a policy of [Full spectrum deterrence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N-deterrence), considering its nuclear program vital for [deterring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_and_its_Nuclear_Deterrent_Program) foreign aggression.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENoor2023-302]&#x20;



Prime Minister [Shehbaz Sharif](6502561.md) at the 2022 [Shanghai Cooperation Organisation](152422.md) summit

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEmbassy_of_the_Russian_Federation_to_the_Republic_of_Malta2022-303]

Located strategically in the world's major maritime oil supply lines and communication [fiber optic corridors](3372377.md), Pakistan also enjoys proximity to the natural resources of Central Asian countries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShah1997-304] Pakistan actively participates in the United Nations with a [Permanent Representative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_Representative_of_Pakistan_to_the_United_Nations) representing its positions in international politics.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWasi2005-305] It has advocated for the concept of "[enlightened moderation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enlightened_moderation)" in the Muslim world.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZahraBouckaertJadoonJabeen2022-306] Pakistan is a member of the Commonwealth of Nations, [SAARC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_Association_for_Regional_Cooperation), [ECO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_Cooperation_Organization),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETurner2016-307][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKemal2004-308] and the [G20 developing nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G20_developing_nations).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoekmanKostecki2009-309]&#x20;

Pakistan is designated as an "Iron Brother" by China, emphasising the significance of their close and supportive [relationship](10480332.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEQingyan2021-310] In the 1950s, Pakistan opposed the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) for geopolitical reasons. During the [Soviet–Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War) in the 1980s, it was a close ally of the United States.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERizvi2004-294] Relations with Russia have improved since the end of the [Cold War](325329.md),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEClary2022-311] but Pakistan's relationship with the United States has been "on-and-off".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERizvi2004-294] Initially a close ally during the Cold War,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarat2007-312] Pakistan's relations with the US soured in the 1990s due to [sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pressler_amendment) over its secretive nuclear program.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMazzetti2013-313] Since [9/11](5058690.md), Pakistan has been a US ally on [counterterrorism](83392.md), but their relationship has been strained due to diverging interests and mistrust during the [20-year war](19666611.md) and terrorism issues. Although Pakistan was granted [major non-NATO ally](3812445.md) status by the U.S. in 2004,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZaidiAhmad2021-314] it faced accusations of supporting the [Taliban insurgents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taliban_insurgency) in Afghanistan.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYousafzai2021-315]&#x20;

Pakistan does not have formal [diplomatic relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan-Israel_relations) with [Israel](9282173.md); nonetheless, an exchange occurred between the two countries in 2005, with Turkey acting as an intermediary.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZelnick2013-316]&#x20;

#### Relations with China

Main article: [China–Pakistan relations](10480332.md)



Pakistani Prime Minister [Huseyn Shaheed Suhrawardy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huseyn_Shaheed_Suhrawardy) with Chinese Premier [Zhou Enlai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhou_Enlai) signing the Treaty of Friendship Between China and Pakistan.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEvan_Tonder2018-317]

&#x20;Pakistan is host to China's largest embassy.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchuurmans202373-318]

Pakistan was among the first nations to establish formal diplomatic ties with China,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen2011-319] forging a strong relationship since China's [1962 conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Indian_War) with India, culminating in a special bond.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchwinghammer2018-320] During the 1970s, Pakistan acted as an intermediary in U.S.-China rapprochement,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAfridiBajoria2010-321] facilitating US President [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon)'s [historic visit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_Nixon_visit_to_China) to China.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoos2024-322][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELordMastroNaftaliBrinkley2022-323] Despite changes in Pakistani governance and regional/global dynamics, China's influence in Pakistan remains paramount.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAfridiBajoria2010-321] In reciprocation, China stands as Pakistan's largest trading partner, with substantial investment in Pakistani infrastructure, notably the [Gwadar port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwadar_port).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERaju2021-324] In 2015 alone, they inked 51 agreements and Memorandums of Understanding (MoUs) for cooperative efforts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERimmer2020-325] Both nations signed a [Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China%E2%80%93Pakistan_Free_Trade_Agreement) in 2006,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZreik2024-326] with China making its largest investment in Pakistan's history through [CPEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CPEC).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDorsey2018-327] Pakistan acts as China's liaison to the Muslim world,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShih2022-328] and both nations support each other on sensitive issues like Kashmir, Taiwan, Xinjiang, and more.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPant2011-329]&#x20;

#### Relations with the Muslim world



Pakistani Prime Minister [Zulfikar Ali Bhutto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zulfikar_Ali_Bhutto) with leaders from [Algeria](358.md), [Bangladesh](3454.md), and [Saudi Arabia](349303.md) in [Lahore](125315.md), February 1974. That year, Pakistan hosted 36 Muslim countries at the [Second Islamic Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Summit_of_the_Organisation_of_Islamic_Cooperation#List_of_summits) of the [Organisation of Islamic Cooperation](47725.md).

After Independence, Pakistan vigorously pursued bilateral relations with other Muslim countries.[^cite_note-Pasha-2005-1-330] The [Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maulana_Muhammad_Ali_Jauhar) brothers sought to project Pakistan as the natural leader of the Islamic world, partly due to its significant manpower and military strength.[^cite_note-Pasha-2005-2-331] [Khaliquzzaman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaudhry_Khaliquzzaman), a prominent [Muslim League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_India_Muslim_League) leader, declared Pakistan's ambition to unite all Muslim countries into [Islamistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamistan), a pan-Islamic entity.[^cite_note-Dhulipala-2015-5-332]&#x20;

These developments, alongside Pakistan's creation, didn't receive approval from the United States, with British Prime Minister [Clement Attlee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clement_Attlee) expressing a hope for India and Pakistan to reunite.[^cite_note-Haqqani-2013-1-333] However, due to a nationalist awakening in the Arab world at that time, there was little interest in Pakistan's Pan-Islamic aspirations.[^cite_note-Haqqani-2013-2-334] Some Arab countries perceived the 'Islamistan' project as Pakistan's bid to dominate other Muslim states.[^cite_note-Roberts-2003-335]&#x20;

Pakistan's founder, [Muhammad Ali Jinnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Ali_Jinnah), consistently advocated for the [Palestinian cause](47246228.md), shaping Pakistan's foreign policy to support Palestinian rights within the broader framework of Muslim solidarity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJafriSultanaIjaz2021-336] During the [1967 Arab-Israel war](29328.md), Pakistan supported the Arab states and played a key role in securing Iran's backing for the Arab cause both within the U.N. and beyond.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAroraGrover1995-337]&#x20;

Pakistan's relations with Iran have been strained by sectarian tensions,[^cite_note-Hunter-2010-338] with both Iran and Saudi Arabia using Pakistan as a battleground for their proxy sectarian war.[^cite_note-Pande-2011-2-339] Since the early days of the [Iran–Iraq war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_war), President [Zia-ul-Haq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zia-ul-Haq) played an important mediatory role, with Pakistan actively engaging in efforts to end the conflict.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETalbot2020-340][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoseHusain1985-341] Pakistan provided support to Saudi Arabia during the [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalladayMateiBruneau2021-342] Pakistan chose to remain neutral during [Operation Decisive Storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Decisive_Storm), refraining from sending military support to Saudi Arabia in its offensive against [Yemen](350939.md). Instead, Pakistan aimed to play a proactive diplomatic role in resolving the crisis,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPanda2019-343] which led to tensions between the two countries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalladayMateiBruneau2021-342] In 2016, Pakistan mediated between Saudi Arabia and Iran following the execution of Shia cleric [Nimr al-Nimr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nimr_al-Nimr), with visits to both countries by then Prime Minister [Nawaz Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nawaz_Sharif) and the Chief of Army Staff, [Raheel Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raheel_Sharif).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBasharat2023-344]&#x20;

Pakistan provided refuge to millions of displaced Afghans after the [Soviet invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War) and supported the [Afghan mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_mujahideen) in their efforts to expel Soviet forces from Afghanistan.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERiedel2010-345] After the Soviets withdrew, [infighting erupted among Mujahideen factions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Civil_War_\(1989%E2%80%931992\)) over control of Afghanistan. Pakistan facilitated [peace talks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peshawar_Accord) to help end the conflict.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVisokaRichmond2022-346] After four years of unresolved conflict between rival Mujahideen groups, Pakistan helped establish the Taliban as a stabilising force.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaizland2023-347] Pakistan's support for the Sunni Taliban in Afghanistan challenged Shia-led Iran, which opposed a Taliban-controlled Afghanistan.[^cite_note-Pande-2011-2-339]&#x20;

Pakistan vigorously advocated for self-determination among Muslims globally. Its efforts in supporting independence movements in countries like Indonesia, Algeria, Tunisia, Morocco, and [Eritrea](17238590.md) fostered strong ties.[^cite_note-Pande-2011-1-348] Due to its support for [Azerbaijan](746.md) in the [Nagorno-Karabakh conflict](4695860.md), Pakistan has not established [diplomatic relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenia%E2%80%93Pakistan_relations) with [Armenia](10918072.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBergKursani2021-349][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBabayevSchochSpanger2019-350]&#x20;

Pakistan and Bangladesh have experienced strained relations, particularly under the [Awami League](404861.md) governments led by [Sheikh Hasina](623439.md), driven by her pro-India stance and historical grievances.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAhmedZahoor2019-351]&#x20;

Pakistan, a prominent member of the [Organisation of Islamic Cooperation](47725.md) (OIC), prioritises maintaining cultural, political, social, and economic relations with Arab and other Muslim-majority nations in its foreign policy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPande2011-352]&#x20;

In September 2025, Pakistan and Saudi Arabia signed a [Strategic Mutual Defence Agreement](81115024.md) under which any aggression against one country is considered an aggression against both, formalising a defensive commitment that also extends Pakistan's nuclear umbrella to Saudi Arabia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKahwaji2025-353]&#x20;

### Kashmir conflict

Main article: [Kashmir conflict](3030955.md)



The areas shown in green are the Pakistani-controlled areas.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECIA1988-354]

[Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir), a Himalayan region at the northern tip of the [Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent), was governed as the autonomous [princely state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princely_state) of [Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_and_Kashmir_\(princely_state\)) during the [British Raj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj) before the [Partition of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_India) in August 1947. This sparked a [major territorial dispute](3030955.md) between India and Pakistan, resulting in [several conflicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_wars_and_conflicts) over the region. India controls about 45.1% of Kashmir, including [Jammu and Kashmir](61449812.md) and [Ladakh](303611.md), while Pakistan controls roughly 38.2%, comprising [Azad Jammu and Kashmir](2745.md) and [Gilgit−Baltistan](18807336.md). Additionally, about 20% of the region, known as [Aksai Chin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aksai_Chin) and the [Shaksgam Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Karakoram_Tract), is under Chinese control.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKudaisyaYong2004-355] India claims the entire Kashmir region based on the [Instrument of Accession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instrument_of_Accession_\(Jammu_and_Kashmir\)) signed by the princely state's ruler, Maharaja Hari Singh,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZahoorRumi2020-356] while Pakistan argues for its Muslim-majority population,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBhattacharyya2023-357] and geographical proximity to Pakistan.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERaghavan2012-358] The United Nations was involved in resolving the conflict, leading to a ceasefire in 1949 and the establishment of the [Line of Control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line_of_Control) (LoC) as a *de facto* border.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStone2017-359] India, fearing Kashmir's secession, did not hold the promised plebiscite, as it believed Kashmiris would vote to join Pakistan.[^cite_note-Endrst-1965-360]&#x20;

Pakistan claims that its position is for the right of the [Kashmiri people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmiris) to determine their future through impartial elections as mandated by the United Nations, while India has stated that Kashmir is an "[integral part](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akhand_Bharat)" of India, referring to the [1972 Simla Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simla_Agreement) and to the fact that [regional elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Jammu_and_Kashmir) take place regularly.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhurshid2016-361] Certain Kashmiri independence groups believe that Kashmir should be independent of both India and Pakistan.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOldenburg2019-362]&#x20;

### Military

Main article: [Pakistan Armed Forces](23369.md)



[Pakistan Air Force](377433.md)'s [JF-17 Thunder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JF-17_Thunder) flying in front of the 8,130-metre-high (26,660-foot) [Nanga Parbat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanga_Parbat)

Pakistan is considered a [middle power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_power) nation,[^cite_note-Middle_power_nation-363][^cite_note-RSCT-364] with the world's [seventh-largest standing armed forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_number_of_military_and_paramilitary_personnel) in terms of personnel size, comprising approximately 660,000 active-duty troops and 291,000 paramilitary personnel as of 2024.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIISS2024-365] [Established in 1947](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_Pakistan), the [armed forces of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armed_forces_of_Pakistan) wielded significant influence over [national politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Pakistan).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBartholomees2008-366] The main branches include the [Army](1965925.md), [Navy](1183789.md), and [Air Force](377433.md), supported by numerous [paramilitaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Armed_Forces).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeRouenHeo2005-367]&#x20;

The [Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chairman_Joint_Chiefs_of_Staff_Committee) (CJCSC) is the highest-ranking military officer, advising the civilian government. However, they lack direct command over the branches and serve as intermediaries, ensuring communication between the military and civilian leadership. Overseeing the [Joint Staff Headquarters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Staff_Headquarters_\(Pakistan\)), they coordinate inter-service cooperation and joint military missions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlood1996287-368]&#x20;

[Command and control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_command_and_control) over Pakistan's [strategic arsenal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan%27s_nuclear_testing_series) development and employment is vested in the [National Command Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Command_Authority_\(Pakistan\)), overseeing work on [nuclear doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_Doctrine_of_Pakistan) to maintain [Full spectrum deterrence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full_spectrum_deterrence).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhan2012-153]&#x20;

The United States, Turkey, and China maintain close military relations with [Pakistan Armed Forces](23369.md), regularly exporting military equipment and [technology transfer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technology_transfer).[^cite_note-Military_relations-369] Pakistan was the 5th-largest recipient and importer of arms between 2019 and 2023.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWezeman_et_al.2024-370]&#x20;

#### Military history

Main article: [Military history of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_Pakistan)

Since 1947, Pakistan has been involved in [four](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_wars_and_conflicts) [conventional wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conventional_warfare) with India.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGatesRoy2016-371] The [first conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pak_war_of_1947) took place in Kashmir and ended in a United Nations-mediated ceasefire, with Pakistan gaining control of one-third of the region.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOetzelTing-Toomey2006-372] Territorial disputes led to [another war in 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_War_of_1965). In 1971, India and Pakistan fought [another war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_War_of_1971) over [East Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Pakistan), with Indian forces aiding its independence, leading to the creation of [Bangladesh](3454.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECenter_for_Preventive_Action2024-373] Tensions in Kargil brought the two countries to the [brink of war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_War_of_1999).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBusch2014-374]&#x20;

Pakistan's primary intelligence agency, the Inter-Services Intelligence (ISI), was established within a year of Pakistan's independence in 1947.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESprague2020-375] During the [Soviet–Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War), Pakistan's intelligence community, mostly the [ISI](2159021.md), [coordinated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Cyclone) [US resources](37258993.md) to support [Afghan mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_mujahideen) and [foreign fighters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Arabs) against Soviet presence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERupert1989-376] The [PAF](377433.md) engaged with [Soviet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Air_Forces) and [Afghan Air Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Air_Force) during the [conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_war_in_afghanistan).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWithington2005-377] Pakistan has been an active [participant in UN peacekeeping missions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_peacekeeping_missions_involving_Pakistan),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_ConingAoiKarlsrud2017-378] playing a major role in operations like the rescue mission in [Mogadishu](60154.md), Somalia, in 1993.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart2002-379] According to a 2023 UN report, the Pakistani military was the fifth largest troop contributor to UN peacekeeping missions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUN2023-380]&#x20;

Pakistan has [deployed its military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Armed_Forces_deployments) in some Arab countries, providing defense, training, and advisory roles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECordesman1987-381][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChengappa2004-382] The [PAF](377433.md)'s fighter pilots participated in missions against Israel during the [Six-Day War](29328.md) and the [Yom Kippur War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yom_Kippur_War).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFaruqui2019-383] Pakistani [special forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Service_Group) assisted [Saudi forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_forces) in [Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mecca) during the [Grand Mosque Seizure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Mosque_Seizure).[^cite_note-Miller-2015-384] Pakistan also sent 5,000 troops as part of a US-led coalition for the defense of Saudi Arabia during the [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERizvi1993-385]&#x20;

Despite the UN arms embargo on [Bosnia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosnia), the ISI under General [Javed Nasir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javed_Nasir) airlifted anti-tank weapons and missiles to Bosnian mujahideen, shifting the tide in favour of Bosnian Muslims. ISI, under Nasir's leadership, supported Chinese Muslims in [Xinjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang), rebel groups in the [Philippines](23440.md), and religious groups in Central Asia.[^cite_note-Wiebes-2003-386][^cite_note-Abbas-2015-387]&#x20;

Since 2001, the Pakistan military has been engaged in [counterinsurgency and internal security operations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_North-West_Pakistan) in the [Khyber Pakhtunkhwa](21950.md) province, primarily targeting [Tehrik-e-Taliban Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehrik-e-Taliban_Pakistan) and associated militant groups. Major military operations conducted during this period include [Operation Enduring Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Enduring_Freedom), [Operation al-Mizan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_al-Mizan), [Operation Zalzala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Zalzala), [Operation Sherdil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Sherdil), [Operation Rah-e-Haq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Rah-e-Haq), [Operation Rah-e-Rast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Rah-e-Rast), and [Operation Rah-e-Nijat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Rah-e-Nijat).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZA_Khan2012-388]&#x20;

### Law enforcement

Main article: [Law enforcement in Pakistan](9371583.md)

See also: [Pakistani Intelligence Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_Intelligence_Community)

[Law enforcement in Pakistan](9371583.md) consists of federal and provincial police agencies. Each of the [four provinces](446017.md) ([Punjab](24751.md), [Sindh](28972.md), [Khyber Pakhtunkhwa](21950.md), and [Balochistan](4687.md)) has its own police force, while the [Islamabad Capital Territory (ICT)](15294.md) has the Islamabad Police.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJaishankar2019-389] Provincial police forces are led by an Inspector-General of Police (IGP), who is appointed from the federally recruited and trained [Police Service of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_Service_of_Pakistan) (PSP) through a consultative process between the federal and provincial governments. All positions above the Assistant Superintendent level are filled from the PSP, ensuring national standards across provincial forces.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKureshiWaseem2024-390]&#x20;

**Specialised Units:**&#x20;

- [National Highways & Motorway Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Highways_%26_Motorway_Police) (NHMP): Enforces traffic laws and ensures safety on Pakistan's inter-provincial motorway network.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEImamFatima2021-391]

- Specialised Rapid Response Units: Specialised counter-terrorism units, such as the [Punjab Elite Police Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elite_Police), have been trained by army commandos and exist in every province to respond to hostage situations and neutralise armed groups.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPeritoParvez2024-392]

The [Civil Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Armed_Forces) (CAF) assist local law enforcement agencies and participate in border security and internal security operations, particularly in conflict-affected regions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWolf2019-393]&#x20;

In 2021, the [National Intelligence Coordination Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Intelligence_Coordination_Committee_\(Pakistan\)) was established to improve coordination among Pakistan's intelligence agencies. The inaugural meeting was attended by the heads of the [ISI](2159021.md), [IB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intelligence_Bureau_\(Pakistan\)), and [FIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Investigation_Agency).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESaeed2021-394]&#x20;

### Crime

Further information: [Crime in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_in_Pakistan)

According to the 2025 [Global Organized Crime Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Organized_Crime_Index), Pakistan had a criminality score of 6.32 out of 10, ranking 45th among 193 countries, and a resilience score ranking 131st among 193 countries, with the latter measuring the country's capacity to withstand and respond to organized crime.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGI-TOC2025-395]&#x20;

According to the [Federal Investigation Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Investigation_Agency)'s annual report, the agency received more than 73,000 cybercrime complaints in 2024, of which 1,604 were registered as cases, with financial fraud accounting for nearly half of all complaints. In 2025, the [National Cyber Crimes Investigation Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Cyber_Crimes_Investigation_Agency) estimated that cybercrime had increased by about 35% compared with the previous year. A 2025 survey conducted on behalf of [Visa Inc.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_Inc.) found that 55% of respondents in Pakistan had fallen victim to an [online scam](805626.md) in 2024, while 20% reported having been victims more than once.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHussain2025-396]&#x20;

Pakistan scored 27 out of 100 on the [Corruption Perceptions Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_Perceptions_Index) in 2024, ranking 135th out of 180 countries in the assessment of perceived public-sector corruption.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECPI2025-397]&#x20;

### Human rights

Main articles: [Human rights in Pakistan](4513212.md) and [LGBT rights in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Pakistan)

In Pakistan, all sexual activity outside of marriage is illegal.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGerber2021-398] The punishment for sex outside marriage (*[zina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zina)*) ranges from up to five years' imprisonment for minors to 100 lashes for unmarried adults and [stoning to death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stoning_to_death) for married adults, depending on marital status, age, sanity, and whether strict evidentiary requirements for a *[hadd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadd)* punishment—such as four adult male Muslim witnesses or a confession—are met; however, no one has been stoned to death under the law to date.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaderin2017-399] Male [homosexuality](5488304.md) is illegal in Pakistan and is punishable by up to ten years in prison, [corporal punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corporal_punishment) such as [whipping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whipping), and, under [Islamic law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_law) since 1990, potentially even stoning.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETin2008-400]&#x20;

[Honour killings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honour_killings), locally referred to as *[karo-kari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karo-kari)* in parts of Pakistan, involve the killing of women and, less frequently, men by family or community members over alleged extramarital relationships or perceived violations of honour. Human rights organisations report that hundreds of women are killed each year, with many cases unreported.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESingh_et_al.2024-401] Pakistan also has a high number of reported [acid attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acid_attacks), with about 200 incidents annually as of 2023, most victims being women. According to [Masarrat Misbah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masarrat_Misbah), founder of the [Depilex Smileagain Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Depilex_Smileagain_Foundation), women have been targeted for rejecting marriage proposals, giving birth to daughters instead of sons, or insufficient dowry. The 2011 Acid and Burn Crime Bill criminalised acid attacks, making them punishable by lengthy imprisonment and fines.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMubasharMir2023-402]&#x20;

In parts of Pakistan, girls are given in marriage to settle disputes or crimes between families in a customary practice called *[Vani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vani_\(custom\))*. Girls married through this practice are often treated as compensation for a dispute, which places them in a vulnerable social position within the family they marry into. In 2021, the [Federal Shariat Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Shariat_Court) declared the practice un-Islamic and unconstitutional, reaffirming earlier Supreme Court jurisprudence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAftab2022-403]&#x20;

[UN Women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Women) reported in 2024 that around one in six girls in Pakistan are married during childhood, with the country home to nearly 19 million child brides.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUN_Women2024-404] In 2025, Pakistan enacted a law setting the minimum legal age for marriage at 18 and introducing penalties for arranging underage marriages; the law is limited to the [Islamabad Capital Territory](15294.md) and does not apply nationwide.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalk_Free2025-405]&#x20;

## Economy

Main articles: [Economy of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Pakistan) and [Economic history of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_Pakistan)

Further information: [Real estate in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_estate_in_Pakistan), [Information technology in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_technology_in_Pakistan), and [Banking in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banking_in_Pakistan)

See also: [Pakistan and the International Monetary Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_and_the_International_Monetary_Fund)

Pakistan's economy [ranks 27th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)) globally by [purchasing power parity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity) (PPP) and [42nd by nominal GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIMF_(PPP)2026-406][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIMF_(nom)2025-408] Historically, Pakistan was part of the wealthiest [region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent) in the first millennium CE, but lost ground to regions like China and Western Europe by the 18th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaddison2006-417] Pakistan is a [developing country](78449.md),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPasillasBrundinMarkowska2017-418] and part of the [Next Eleven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Next_Eleven), poised to become one of the world's largest economies in the 21st century, alongside the [BRIC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BRIC_\(economics_term\)) countries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEComimAnandFennell2021-419] The semi-industrialised economy is heavily dependent on agriculture, and industrial growth in Pakistan benefits significantly from agricultural expansion.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHenneberryKhanPiewthongngam2000-420] Pakistan's economy has shifted from [agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Pakistan) to trade and services, with agriculture contributing about one-fifth of GDP and manufacturing one-sixth, while trade and services form the largest share.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurkiTeekahZiring2026-421] In 2023, Pakistan was the 66th-largest export economy,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOEC2023-422] with a trade deficit of US\$21.3 billion.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWorld_Bank_(WITS)2025-423]&#x20;



A poor boy in [Balochistan](4687.md), around 44.7% of the population live below the international poverty line in Pakistan

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIqbal2025-424]



Handcrafts seller girl in [Sindh](28972.md), 16.5% of Pakistani population live in extreme poverty on less than US\$3 a day

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIqbal2025-424]

As of 2025, Pakistan's [nominal GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nominal_GDP) stood at US\$407.79 billion,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIMF_(nom)2025-408] while GDP ([PPP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity)) was US\$1.8 trillion in 2026.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIMF_(PPP)2026-406] The nominal per capita GDP was US\$1,700,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIMF_(nom_per_capita)2025-409] and the [GDP (PPP)/capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GDP_\(PPP\)_per_capita) amounted to US\$6,950 ([international dollars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_dollar)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIMF_(PPP_per_capita)2026-407] Around 44.7% of the population live below the international poverty line of US\$4.20 a day, 16.5% live in extreme poverty on less than US\$3 a day,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIqbal2025-424] 8% of the total labour force is unemployed, and [CPI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumer_price_index) inflation in 2025 was 4.5%.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIMF_(datasets)2026-410] Pakistan ranks near the bottom globally on the [Global Gender Gap Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Gender_Gap_Index)'s economic participation and opportunity sub-index for women, reflecting very low female labour force participation and limited representation of women in senior economic roles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWEF2025-425] Pakistan's middle class is estimated to be around 40–50 million people, representing 15–20 per cent of the population.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAhmad2024-426] Among [South Asian](21566765.md) countries, Pakistan's GDP growth rate of 2.5% in 2024 was the second lowest after Afghanistan.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEADB2025a-427]&#x20;

Pakistan has strong potential to produce diverse food commodities due to its vast natural resource base across varied ecological and climatic zones.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESharifJavedAbbasHassan2008-428] It has the world's 7th-largest [labour force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_in_Pakistan),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConte2025-429] and its [diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_diaspora) of around 10 million,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbbas2026-430] sent US\$38.3 billion in remittances in fiscal year 2024–25, as reported by the [Special Investment Facilitation Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Investment_Facilitation_Council).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESIFC2025-431] Pakistan's share of global exports averaged 0.14% between 1990 and 2023 and was 0.12% in 2023.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Global_Economy2023-432]&#x20;

### Services

As of 2025–26, the services sector was the largest contributor to Pakistan's GDP, accounting for 58.42% of GDP.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoF2026-433] The sector accounted for 41.6% of total employment in 2024–25.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPBS2026-434] In the [International Telecommunication Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Telecommunication_Union)'s [ICT Development Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ICT_Development_Index), Pakistan's score increased from 56.4 in 2025 to 67.7 in 2026, an increase of 20% from 2025.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEITU2026-435] Approximately 57% of Pakistan's population used the Internet in 2024.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEISP2026-436] In fiscal year 2023–24, Pakistan's ICT exports rose to a record \$2.6 billion.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJahanzaib2026-437] Pakistan ranks among the world's top five freelancing markets,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhan2025-438] with an estimated three million freelancers. During the first ten months of fiscal year 2025–26, freelancers earned more than US\$950 million in foreign exchange.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDawn2026-439]&#x20;

### Agriculture

Main article: [Agriculture in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Pakistan)



Pakistan is the 6th largest producer of [dates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Date_\(fruit\)) in the world.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaderSiddiqAleid2013-440]

The agriculture sector employs 43.5% of the labour force and is a major source of foreign exchange.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhanKhan2018-441] Pakistan produced 28.98 million metric tons of wheat in 2025/2026 (3% of global output), ranking 7th globally.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUSDA2026-442] Pakistan ranks fourth in cotton production,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAkınGürsoy2024-443] fifth in sugar production,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESaeedSuleriaBader_Ul_AinTufail2026-444] and is the fourth-largest milk producer globally. Though land and water resources haven't increased proportionately, productivity gains, especially from the [Green Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Revolution) in the late 1960s and 1970s, significantly boosted wheat and rice yields. Private tube wells and High Yielding Varieties (HYVs) further augmented crop yields.[^cite_note-IBA-445] Meat industry accounts for 1.4 percent of overall GDP.[^cite_note-446]&#x20;

### Industry

Main article: [Industry of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industry_of_Pakistan)

See also: [Textile industry in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textile_industry_in_Pakistan)

Industry, constituting 19.74% of GDP and 24% of total employment, is the second-largest sector. Large-scale manufacturing (LSM) dominates, representing 12.2% of GDP, with cement production thriving due to demand from Afghanistan and the domestic real estate sector.[^cite_note-447] In 2013, Pakistan exported 7,708,557 metric tons of cement, with an installed capacity of 44,768,250 metric tons.[^cite_note-448] The [textile industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textile_industry_in_Pakistan), a key player in Pakistan's manufacturing, contributes 9.5% to GDP and employs around 15 million people. As of 2022, Pakistan ranks seventh globally in cotton production,[^cite_note-449] with substantial spinning capacity, making it a major exporter of textile products in Asia.[^cite_note-450] China has been a significant buyer of Pakistani textiles, importing US\$1.527 billion worth of textiles in 2012.[^cite_note-451]&#x20;

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Pakistan)



[Shangrila Lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shangrila_Lake) and adjoining resort in [Skardu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skardu), [Gilgit-Baltistan](18807336.md)

Pakistan drew around 6.6 million foreign tourists in 2018.[^cite_note-452] Pakistan boasts attractions from [mangroves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_River_Delta-Arabian_Sea_mangroves) in the south to Himalayan [hill stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_hill_stations_of_Pakistan) in the northeast, including ancient Buddhist ruins of [Takht-i-Bahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Takht-i-Bahi) and [Taxila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxila), the 5,000-year-old [Indus Valley civilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Valley_civilisation) sites such as [Mohenjo-daro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohenjo-daro) and [Harappa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harappa),[^cite_note-453] and numerous [mountain peaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mountains_in_Pakistan) over 7,000 metres (23,000 feet).[^cite_note-454] The northern part of Pakistan boasts numerous old fortresses, showcasing ancient architecture. It encompasses the [Hunza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunza_Valley) and [Chitral valleys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chitral_valley), where the small pre-Islamic [Kalasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalash_people) community resides, claiming descent from Alexander the Great.[^cite_note-Bezhan2017-455] [Lahore](125315.md), Pakistan's cultural capital, contains lots of [Mughal architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_architecture), including the [Badshahi Masjid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badshahi_Masjid), the [Shalimar Gardens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shalimar_Gardens_\(Lahore\)), the [Tomb of Jahangir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tomb_of_Jahangir), and the [Lahore Fort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahore_Fort). Following the [2005 Kashmir earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_Kashmir_earthquake), *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* highlighted "The top five tourist sites in Pakistan" to boost tourism, featuring destinations like Taxila, Lahore, the [Karakoram Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karakoram_Highway), [Karimabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karimabad_\(Hunza\)), and [Lake Saiful Muluk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Saiful_Muluk).[^cite_note-456] Festivals and government initiatives aim to promote Pakistan's cultural heritage.[^cite_note-457] In 2015, the [World Economic Forum](172185.md) ranked Pakistan 125th out of 141 countries in its Travel & Tourism Competitiveness Report.[^cite_note-458]&#x20;

### Natural resources

Main articles: [Fuel extraction in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuel_extraction_in_Pakistan) and [Mining in Pakistan](28179941.md)



[Surface mining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surface_mining) in [Sindh](28972.md)

Pakistan's untapped coal reserves have been estimated as the fifth largest in the world and equivalent to about 400 billion barrels of oil, exceeding the combined oil reserves of Saudi Arabia and Iran.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDawn2009b-459] [Daniel Fitzgerald Runde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Fitzgerald_Runde) wrote in *[Forbes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes)* in 2016 that Pakistan "could be the 'Saudi Arabia of Coal'."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERunde2016-460]&#x20;

The [Reko Diq Mine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reko_Diq_Mine) has one of the world's largest undeveloped [copper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copper) deposits, with an estimated 15 million tonnes in reserves,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMining.com2026-461] and is expected to become the world's fifth largest copper mine upon completion;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEADB2025b-462] a feasibility study also estimates that it could produce approximately 17.9 million ounces of gold over its projected 37-year mine life, with total copper and gold reserves valued at over \$60 billion at prevailing prices.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKiani2025-463]&#x20;

## Infrastructure

See also: [Water supply and sanitation in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_supply_and_sanitation_in_Pakistan)

Pakistan was lauded as the top nation for infrastructure development in South Asia during the 2016 annual meetings of the IMF and World Bank.[^cite_note-464]&#x20;

### Power and energy

Main articles: [Nuclear power in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_power_in_Pakistan), [Energy in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Pakistan), and [Electricity sector in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electricity_sector_in_Pakistan)



[Tarbela Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarbela_Dam), the largest earth filled dam in the world, was constructed in 1968.

As of May 2021, Pakistan operates six licensed commercial [nuclear power plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_power_plants).[^cite_note-465] The [Pakistan Atomic Energy Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Atomic_Energy_Commission) (PAEC) oversees these plants, while the [Pakistan Nuclear Regulatory Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Nuclear_Regulatory_Authority) ensures their safe operation.[^cite_note-466] These plants contribute approximately 5.8% to Pakistan's electricity supply, while fossil fuels (crude oil and natural gas) provide 64.2%, [hydroelectric power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroelectric_power) provides 29.9%, and [coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal_power_plant) contributes 0.1%.[^cite_note-Express_Tribune,_2014-467][^cite_note-468] The [KANUPP-I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Nuclear_Power_Plant), Pakistan's first commercial nuclear power plant, was supplied by Canada in 1971. Sino-Pakistani nuclear cooperation began in the 1980s, leading to the establishment of CHASNUPP-I. In 2005, both countries proposed a joint energy security plan, aiming for a generation capacity exceeding 160,000 [MWe](21347693.md) by 2030. Pakistan's Nuclear Energy Vision 2050 targets a capacity of 40,000 MWe,[^cite_note-469] with 8,900 MWe expected by 2030.[^cite_note-470]&#x20;

In June 2008, the nuclear complex at [Chashma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chashma_Nuclear_Power_Plant) in Punjab Province expanded with the installation of Chashma-III and Chashma–IV reactors, each with 325–340 MWe, costing [₨](588111.md)129 billion, with ₨80 billion from international sources, mainly China. Another agreement for China's assistance was signed in October 2008, seen as a response to the [US–India agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India%E2%80%93United_States_Civil_Nuclear_Agreement). The project's cost was then US\$1.7 billion, with a foreign loan of US\$1.07 billion. In 2013, Pakistan established a second [nuclear complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Nuclear_Power_Complex) in Karachi with plans for additional reactors, similar to Chashma.[^cite_note-471] [Electrical energy in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electricity_in_Pakistan) is generated by [various corporations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electricity_distribution_companies_of_Pakistan) and distributed evenly among the [four provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Provinces_\(Pakistan\)) by the [National Electric Power Regulatory Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Electric_Power_Regulatory_Authority) (NEPRA). However, Karachi-based [K-Electric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K-Electric) and [Water and Power Development Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_and_Power_Development_Authority) (WAPDA) generate much of the electricity used in Pakistan and collect revenue nationwide.[^cite_note-472] In 2023, Pakistan's installed [electricity generation](9540.md) capacity was \~45,885 MWt.[^cite_note-473] Pakistan produced 1,135 megawatts of [renewable energy](25784.md) for the month of October 2016. Pakistan expects to produce 10,000 megawatts of renewable energy by 2025.[^cite_note-474]&#x20;

### Transport

Main article: [Transport in Pakistan](23368.md)



The motorway passes through the [Salt Range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salt_Range) mountains.



[Karachi Cantonment railway station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Cantonment_railway_station)

Pakistan boasts 2567 km of motorways and approximately 263,942 km of highways, which handle 92% of passengers and 96% of freight traffic. Despite constituting only 4.6% of the total road length, these north–south links manage 85% of the nation's traffic. They connect southern seaports such as [Karachi port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Karachi) and [Port Qasim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Qasim) in Sindh, along with [Gwadar Port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwadar_port) and [Port of Pasni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Pasni) in Balochistan, to populous provinces like Punjab and Khyber Pakhtunkhwa domestically, and neighbouring countries like Afghanistan, [Central Asia](6742.md), and China through the [China Pakistan Economic Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Pakistan_Economic_Corridor).[^cite_note-nation-475][^cite_note-pc-476][^cite_note-477][^cite_note-478] According to the [WEF](172185.md)'s Global Competitiveness Report, Pakistan's port infrastructure quality ratings rose from 3.7 to 4.1 between 2007 and 2016.[^cite_note-479] The railway's share of inland traffic is reduced to below 8% for passengers and 4% for freight.[^cite_note-DSEC-480] This shift led to a decrease in total rail track from 8,775 kilometres (5,453 miles) in 1990–91 to 7,791 kilometres (4,841 miles) in 2011.[^cite_note-pc-476][^cite_note-nation-475]&#x20;



[Karakoram Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karakoram_Highway), connecting Pakistan to China, is one of the highest paved roads in the world.

The transport landscape of Pakistan features various [modern transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_transit) systems. The [Orange Line Metro Train](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange_Line_\(Lahore_Metro\)) in Lahore, inaugurated in 2020,[^cite_note-481] spans 27.1 km (16.8 mi),[^cite_note-482] and includes both elevated and underground sections, accommodating over 250,000 passengers daily.[^cite_note-483] Lahore also boasts the [Lahore Metrobus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahore_Metrobus), the first of its kind in Pakistan, operational since February 2013.[^cite_note-484] The [Rawalpindi-Islamabad Metrobus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rawalpindi-Islamabad_Metrobus), stretching 48.1 km, commenced its first phase in June 2015, with subsequent extensions, and employs e-ticketing and an Intelligent Transportation System.[^cite_note-Orange_line_trial_run-485][^cite_note-PM_Shehbaz_Sharif_inaugurates_metrobus_service-486] [Multan Metrobus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multan_Metrobus), inaugurated in January 2017, serves [Multan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multan) with its rapid transit services.[^cite_note-487][^cite_note-488] [Peshawar's Bus Rapid Transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peshawar_Metrobus), inaugurated in August 2020, marks the fourth BRT system in Pakistan. Karachi's [Green Line Metrobus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Line_-_Karachi_Metrobus), operational since December 2021, is part of a larger metrobus project financed by the Government of Pakistan and initiated in February 2016.[^cite_note-489][^cite_note-490][^cite_note-491] Meanwhile, [Faisalabad](401880.md) awaits its proposed rapid transit project, the [Faisalabad Metrobus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faisalabad_Metrobus).[^cite_note-492] [Karachi Circular Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karachi_Circular_Railway), partially revived in November 2020, offers public transit services in the Karachi metropolitan area.[^cite_note-493][^cite_note-494] Additionally, plans are underway to resurrect Karachi's [tramway service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohamedali_Tramways_Company), which ceased operations in 1975, in collaboration with Austrian experts.[^cite_note-495][^cite_note-496]&#x20;

As of 2013, Pakistan boasts approximately 151 airports and airfields, encompassing both military and [civilian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Civil_Aviation_Authority) installations.[^cite_note-497] Despite [Jinnah International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jinnah_International_Airport) serving as the primary international gateway, significant international traffic also flows through [Lahore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allama_Iqbal_International_Airport), [Islamabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamabad_International_Airport), [Peshawar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peshawar_International_Airport), [Quetta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quetta_International_Airport), [Faisalabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faisalabad_International_Airport), [Sialkot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sialkot_International_Airport), and [Multan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multan_International_Airport) airports. The civil aviation industry, deregulated in 1993, operates with a blend of [public](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalisation_in_Pakistan) and [private](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privatisation_in_Pakistan) entities while [state-owned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_owned_enterprises) [Pakistan International Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_International_Airlines) (PIA) dominates, carrying 73% of domestic passengers and all domestic freight.&#x20;

### Science and technology

Main articles: [Science and technology in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_and_technology_in_Pakistan) and [List of Pakistani inventions and discoveries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Pakistani_inventions_and_discoveries)



[Abdus Salam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdus_Salam) won the 1979 [Nobel Prize in Physics](52497.md) for his contribution to [electroweak interaction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electroweak_interaction). He was the first Muslim to win a Nobel prize in science.



[Atta-ur-Rahman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atta_ur_Rahman_\(scientist\)) won the [UNESCO Science Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO_Science_Prize) for pioneering contributions in chemistry in 1999, the first Muslim to win it.

Developments in [science and technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_and_technology_in_Pakistan) have played a significant role in Pakistan's infrastructure, linking the nation to the global community.[^cite_note-498] Each year, the [Pakistan Academy of Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Academy_of_Sciences) and the government invite scientists worldwide to the [International Nathiagali Summer College on Physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Nathiagali_Summer_College_on_Physics).[^cite_note-499] In 2005, Pakistan hosted an international seminar on "Physics in Developing Countries" for the International Year of Physics.[^cite_note-500] Pakistani theoretical physicist [Abdus Salam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdus_Salam) won a [Nobel Prize in Physics](52497.md) for his work on the [electroweak interaction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electroweak_interaction).[^cite_note-501] Pakistani scientists have made notable contributions in mathematics, biology, economics, [computer science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer_science), and [genetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetics).[^cite_note-502]&#x20;

In [chemistry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemistry), [Salimuzzaman Siddiqui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salimuzzaman_Siddiqui) identified the medicinal properties of the [neem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neem) tree's components.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMuniapanShaikh2007-503][^cite_note-504] [Ayub K. Ommaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayub_K._Ommaya) developed the [Ommaya reservoir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ommaya_reservoir) for treating brain conditions.[^cite_note-505] Scientific research is integral to [Pakistani universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_universities), national laboratories, [science parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_park), and the [industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industry_of_Pakistan).[^cite_note-506] [Abdul Qadeer Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Qadeer_Khan) spearheaded Pakistan's [HEU](37555.md)-based [gas-centrifuge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zippe-type_centrifuge) [uranium enrichment](37555.md) program for its [atomic bomb project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_and_weapons_of_mass_destruction).[^cite_note-507] He established the [Kahuta Research Laboratories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kahuta_Research_Laboratories) (KRL) in 1976, serving as both its senior scientist and the Director-General until his retirement in 2001. Besides atomic bomb project, he made significant contributions in [molecular morphology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morphology_\(biology\)), physical [martensite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martensite), and their applications in [condensed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Condensed_matter_physics) and [material physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Material_physics).[^cite_note-508]&#x20;

In 2023, Pakistan ranked 26th globally in published scientific papers.[^cite_note-509] The influential [Pakistan Academy of Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Academy_of_Sciences) guides the government on science policies.[^cite_note-510] Pakistan was ranked 99th in the [Global Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index) by 2025.[^cite_note-511][^cite_note-512]&#x20;

The 1960s marked the rise of Pakistan's space program, led by [SUPARCO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_and_Upper_Atmosphere_Research_Commission), yielding advancements in [rocketry](26301.md), [electronics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronics_Engineering), and [aeronomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aeronomy). Notably, Pakistan launched its [first rocket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rehbar-I) into space, pioneering South Asia's space exploration.[^cite_note-513] In 1990, it successfully launched its [first satellite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badr-1), becoming the first Muslim nation and second in South Asia to achieve this milestone.[^cite_note-514]&#x20;

> Pakistan witnessed a fourfold increase in its scientific productivity in the past decade surging from approximately 2,000 articles per year in 2006 to more than 9,000 articles in 2015. Making Pakistan's cited article's higher than the [BRIC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BRIC_\(economics_term\)) countries put together.&#x20;

— [Thomson Reuters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomson_Reuters)'s Another BRIC in the Wall 2016 report

[^cite_note-515]

Following the [1971 war with India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_War_of_1971), Pakistan hastily developed [atomic weapons](21785.md) to deter foreign intervention and entered the [atomic age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_age).[^cite_note-United_Book_Press.-516] Tensions with India led to Pakistan's [1998 underground](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_nuclear_weapons_tests_of_Pakistan) [nuclear tests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chagai-I), making it the [seventh country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_club) to possess such weapons.[^cite_note-517]&#x20;

Pakistan is the sole Muslim nation active in Antarctica research, maintaining its [Jinnah Antarctic Research Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Antarctic_Programme) since 1992.[^cite_note-518] The government invests heavily in [information technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_technology_in_Pakistan) projects, focusing on e-government and infrastructure.[^cite_note-519]&#x20;

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Pakistan)

Further information: [Overseas Pakistani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_Pakistani)



Population Density per square kilometre of each Pakistani District as of the 2017 Pakistan Census



Population of each Pakistani District as of the 2017 Pakistan Census

This section is an excerpt from [Demographics of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Pakistan).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Demographics_of_Pakistan\&action=edit)]

Pakistan had a population of 247,541,947 according to the final results of the [2023 Pakistani census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Pakistani_census).[^cite_note-Demographics_of_Pakistan_2023Census-520][^cite_note-521][^cite_note-Demographics_of_Pakistan_Area4-522] Pakistan is the world's [fifth–most populous country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_population) and has the [second-largest Muslim population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_by_country#Countries) as of 2023.[^cite_note-Demographics_of_Pakistan_2023census-523][^cite_note-Demographics_of_Pakistan_UNWPP2019-524][^cite_note-525] Between 1951 and 2023, Pakistan's population expanded over sevenfold, going from 33.7 million to 247.5 million. The country has a relatively high, although declining, growth rate supported by high birth rates and low death rates. Between 1998 and 2017, the average annual population growth rate stood at +2.40%.[^cite_note-526]&#x20;

Dramatic social changes have led to [urbanization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbanisation_in_Pakistan) and the emergence of two [megacities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megacity): [Karachi](17123.md) and [Lahore](125315.md). The country's urban population more than tripled between 1981 and 2017 (from 23.8 million to 75.7 million), as Pakistan's [urbanisation rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbanization) rose from 28.2% to 36.4%. Even with this, the nation's urbanisation rate remains one of [the lowest in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbanization_by_sovereign_state), and in 2017, over 130 million Pakistanis (making up nearly 65% of the population) lived in rural areas.&#x20;

Due to a high [fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_fertility_rate), which was estimated at 3.5 in 2022, Pakistan has one of the world's [youngest populations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_median_age). The 2017 census recorded that 40.3% of the country's population was under the age of 15, while only 3.7% of Pakistanis were aged 65 or more.[^cite_note-Demographics_of_Pakistan_2017CensusAge-527] The [median age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median_age) of the country was 19,[^cite_note-Demographics_of_Pakistan_2017CensusAge-527] while its [sex ratio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_ratio) was recorded to be 105 males per 100 females.[^cite_note-Demographics_of_Pakistan_2017CensusPopulation-528] Pakistan ranked last (148th of 148 countries) on the [Global Gender Gap Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Gender_Gap_Report), with a gender parity score of 56.7%, reflecting one of the lowest levels of gender equality globally.[^cite_note-529]&#x20;

The [demographic history of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographic_history_of_Pakistan) from the ancient [Indus Valley civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Valley_Civilisation) to the modern era includes the arrival and settlement of many cultures and ethnic groups in the modern region of Pakistan from [Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia) and the nearby [Middle East](19323.md). Because of this, Pakistan has a multicultural, multilinguistic, and multiethnic society. Pakistan is also thought to have the world's fourth-largest [refugee population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_by_refugee_population), estimated at 1.4 million in mid-2021 by the [UNHCR](31964.md).[^cite_note-Demographics_of_Pakistan_RefugeesUNHCR-530]&#x20;

### Urbanisation

Main article: [Urbanisation in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbanisation_in_Pakistan)

Since [independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_of_Pakistan) due to the [partition of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_India), [urbanisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbanisation_in_Pakistan) has surged for various reasons. In the south, [Karachi](17123.md) stands as the most populous commercial hub along the [Indus River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_River).[^cite_note-The_Urban_Frontier—Karachi-531] In the east, west, and north, a dense population arc spans cities like [Lahore](125315.md), [Faisalabad](401880.md), [Rawalpindi](343524.md), [Islamabad](51153.md), [Sargodha](1738747.md), [Gujranwala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gujranwala), [Sialkot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sialkot), [Gujrat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gujrat_city), [Jhelum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jhelum), [Sheikhupura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikhupura), [Nowshera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nowshera,_Khyber-Pakhtunkhwa), [Mardan](1844876.md), and [Peshawar](66230.md). By 1990–2008, city dwellers constituted 36% of Pakistan's population, making it South Asia's most urbanised nation, with over 50% living in towns of 5,000+ inhabitants.[^cite_note-Jason_Burke-532] [Immigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_Pakistan), both domestic and international, significantly fuels urban growth. Migration from India, especially to Karachi, the largest metropolis, and from nearby countries, accelerates urbanisation, posing new political and socio-economic challenges. Economic shifts like the green revolution and political developments also play crucial roles.[^cite_note-Clark-533]&#x20;
