### Ethnicity and languages

Main articles: [Languages of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Pakistan) and [Ethnic groups of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_of_Pakistan)

See also: [Pakistanis](28171281.md)

Pakistan is a diverse society with estimates suggesting it has between 75 and 85 languages.[^cite_note-535][^cite_note-GlottologPK-536] Urdu and English serve as the official languages, with Urdu being the country's [lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca) and a unifying force among over 75% of Pakistanis.[^cite_note-537][^cite_note-2017_Census-538] According to the [2023 national census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Pakistani_census), the largest [ethnolinguistic groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnolinguistic_group) include the [Punjabis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabis) (36.98%), [Pashtuns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtuns) (18.15%), [Sindhis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindhis) (14.31%), [Saraikis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saraiki_people) (12%), [Urdu speaking people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu_speaking_people) (9.25%), [Balochs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baloch_people) (3.38%), [Hindkowans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindkowans)/[Hazarewals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hazarewal) (2.32%), and [Brahuis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahuis) (1.16%).[^cite_note-539][^cite_note-auto-540] The remaining population consists of various ethnic minorities such as [Kashmiris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmiris), [Paharis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pahari_people_\(Kashmir\)), [Chitralis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chitralis), various peoples of [Gilgit-Baltistan](18807336.md), [Kohistanis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Kohistani_people), [Torwalis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torwali_people), [Meos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meo_\(ethnic_group\)), [Hazaras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hazaras), [Kalash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalash_people) and [Siddis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siddi).[^cite_note-EB-Online-541][^cite_note-542] The Pakistani diaspora, numbering over seven million, is the sixth largest in the world.[^cite_note-ribune-2016-543]&#x20;

### Immigration

Main article: [Immigration to Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_Pakistan)



Afghan children near [Islamabad](51153.md) fetching water from water pump. Pakistan once held the second largest refugee population globally after [Turkey](11125639.md).

[^cite_note-544]

Even after the 1947 partition, the Muslims from India kept migrating to Pakistan, especially Karachi and Sindh province.[^cite_note-545] Wars in neighbouring Afghanistan in the 1980s and 1990s pushed millions of [Afghan refugees](3657017.md) into Pakistan, mainly in Khyber-Pakhtunkhwa and [tribal areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federally_Administered_Tribal_Areas), with some in Karachi and Quetta. Pakistan hosts one of the world's largest refugee populations.[^cite_note-546] Additionally, around 2 million [Bengalis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengalis_in_Pakistan) and half a million undocumented individuals, purportedly from [Myanmar](19457.md), reside in Pakistan.[^cite_note-547] Pakistan ordered the [mass expulsion of undocumented Afghans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deportation_of_undocumented_Afghans_from_Pakistan) in October 2023, citing national security risks.[^cite_note-548]&#x20;

Migration of Bengalis and [Rohingya](1918632.md) to Pakistan started in the 1980s and continued till 1998. Karachi hosts a significant number of Bengali settlements, and large Rohingya migration made it one of their largest populations outside Myanmar.[^cite_note-549] Karachi's [Burmese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burmese_people) community resides in various slums across the city.[^cite_note-550]&#x20;

According to [BBC](37627.md), thousands of [Uyghur](31783.md) Muslims live in Gilgit-Baltistan, some left Xinjiang, China and the thriving trading town of Kashgar in 1949, while others are later arrivals, claiming to escape political oppression.[^cite_note-551] Since 1989, thousands of [Kashmiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmiris) Muslim refugees fled to Pakistan, alleging rape and forced displacement by Indian soldiers.[^cite_note-552]&#x20;

### Diaspora

Main article: [Overseas Pakistani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_Pakistani)

According to the UN Department of Economic and Social Affairs, Pakistan has the sixth-largest diaspora globally.[^cite_note-ribune-2016-543] Approximately 7 million Pakistanis reside abroad, mainly in the Middle East, Europe, and North America.[^cite_note-553] Pakistan ranks 10th globally for remittances sent home.[^cite_note-overseaspakistanis1-554][^cite_note-worldbank1-555] Saudi Arabia is the largest source of remittances, contributing \$5.9 billion as of 2016.[^cite_note-556] The term *[Overseas Pakistani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_Pakistani)* is officially recognised by the Government of Pakistan, with the [Ministry of Overseas Pakistanis and Human Resource Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Overseas_Pakistanis) addressing their needs, welfare, and issues. Overseas Pakistanis constitute the second-largest source of foreign exchange remittances to Pakistan, with remittances increasing by over 100% from US\$8.9 billion in 2009–10 to US\$19.9 billion in 2015–16.[^cite_note-remit-557][^cite_note-worldbank1-555]&#x20;

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Pakistan)

| Religions |  | Percent |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Pakistan) |  | 96.3% |
| [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_Pakistan) |  | 2.2% |
| [Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Pakistan) |  | 1.4% |
| [others](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Pakistan#Demographics_of_religion_in_Pakistan) |  | 0.1% |

[Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Pakistan) is the state religion,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMunir1975-230] with freedom of religion guaranteed by the [constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Pakistan).[^cite_note-CoP-559][^cite_note-PakConstitution-560] The majority are Muslims (96.35%), followed by [Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_Pakistan) (2.17%) and [Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Pakistan) (1.37%). Other minorities include [Sikhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhism_in_Pakistan), [Buddhists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Pakistan), [Jains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jainism_in_Pakistan), [Zoroastrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroastrianism) ([Parsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parsis)), and the unique [Kalash people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalash_people) who practice [animism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animism).[^cite_note-Bezhan2017-455] In 2012, 2% of the population identified as atheist in a [Gallup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup_Pakistan) survey.[^cite_note-561]&#x20;

#### Islam

Main article: [Islam in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Pakistan)



[Faisal Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faisal_Mosque), built in 1986 by Turkish architect [Vedat Dalokay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vedat_Dalokay) on behalf of [King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Saud) [Faisal bin Abdul-Aziz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faisal_bin_Abdul-Aziz) of Saudi Arabia

Islam dominates in Pakistan, with about 96.35% of the population being Muslim.[^cite_note-562] Pakistan ranks second globally in Muslim population,[^cite_note-563] and is home to 10.5% of the world's Muslims.[^cite_note-564] Karachi is the largest Muslim city in the world.[^cite_note-565]&#x20;

The majority follow [Sunni Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam), with a significant presence of [Sufism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufism_in_Pakistan), while Shia Muslims constitute a minority.[^cite_note-LoC2-566][^cite_note-567] Shias represent between 5–25%.[^cite_note-LoC2-566][^cite_note-ciafactbook-568][^cite_note-569] The Shia population in Pakistan was estimated at 42 million in 2019.[^cite_note-570] As of 2012, 12% of Pakistani Muslims self-identify as [non-denominational Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-denominational_Muslims).[^cite_note-571]&#x20;

The [Ahmadis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmadiyya_in_Pakistan) are a minority, officially considered non-Muslims.[^cite_note-572][^cite_note-StateDept2008-573] Ahmadis face persecution, banned from calling themselves Muslims since 1974.[^cite_note-574]&#x20;

#### Hinduism

Main article: [Hinduism in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_Pakistan)



Hindu proportion of each Pakistani District in 2017 according to the Pakistan Bureau of Statistics

[Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_Pakistan) is the second-largest religion, followed by 2.17% of the population according to the census in 2023.[^cite_note-2023_Census-558] Pakistan had the fifth-largest Hindu population globally in 2010.[^cite_note-575] In 2023, Hindus numbered 5,217,216.[^cite_note-2023_Census-558][^cite_note-576] They reside across Pakistan but are concentrated in [Sindh](28972.md), where they make up 8.81% of the population.[^cite_note-2023_Census-558] [Umerkot district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umerkot_district) of the province is the only Hindu majority area. [Tharparkar district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tharparkar_district) hosts the largest Hindu population. Four districts – Umerkot, Tharparkar, [Mirpurkhas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirpurkhas_District), and [Sanghar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanghar_District) – have over half of Pakistan's Hindus.[^cite_note-577]&#x20;

At Pakistan's inception, the 'hostage theory' suggested fair treatment of Hindus to safeguard Muslims in India.[^cite_note-578][^cite_note-Dhulipala-2015-6-579] However, some Pakistani Hindus felt marginalised, leading to emigration to India.[^cite_note-bbc200703022-580] In the aftermath of the [Babri Masjid demolition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babri_Masjid_demolition) they faced violence.[^cite_note-581]&#x20;

#### Christianity and other religions

Main article: [Christianity in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Pakistan)



Christian proportion of each Pakistani District in 2017 according to the Pakistan Bureau of Statistics

Christians are the next largest religious minority after Hindus, constituting 1.37% of the population.[^cite_note-2023_Census-558] They are concentrated in [Lahore District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahore_District) (5%) and [Islamabad Capital Territory](15294.md) (over 4%). Karachi hosts a historic [Roman Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholicism_in_Pakistan) community established by [Goan](40010153.md) and [Tamil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamil_people) migrants during British colonial rule.[^cite_note-Districtwise-582]&#x20;

Following Christianity, the [Bahá'í Faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%27%C3%AD_Faith_in_Pakistan) had 30,000 followers in 2008, followed by Sikhism, Buddhism, and [Zoroastrianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroastrianism), each with around 20,000 adherents in 2008,[^cite_note-StateDept2008-573] alongside a small [Jain community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jainism_in_Pakistan).&#x20;

### Education

Main article: [Education in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Pakistan)

See also: [Higher Education Commission (Pakistan)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Higher_Education_Commission_\(Pakistan\)) and [Rankings of universities in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rankings_of_universities_in_Pakistan)



[NUST](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NUST_School_of_Electrical_Engineering_and_Computer_Science) in Islamabad is a top-ranked engineering university.

Pakistan's [constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Pakistan) mandates [free](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_education) primary and secondary education,[^cite_note-PakConstitution-560][^cite_note-583] with public universities established in each province, including [Punjab University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_the_Punjab), [Sindh University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindh_University), [Peshawar University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Peshawar), [Karachi University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Karachi), and [Balochistan University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Balochistan). The country's educational landscape encompasses both [public](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_university) and [private](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_university) universities, fostering collaboration to enhance research and [higher education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Higher_education_in_Pakistan) opportunities, albeit with concerns regarding teaching quality in newer institutions.[^cite_note-584] [Technical and vocational institutions](56551.md) in Pakistan number approximately 3,193,[^cite_note-edu2-585] complemented by *[madrassahs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrassas_in_Pakistan)* providing free Islamic education to students,[^cite_note-586] with government efforts to [regulate and monitor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_reform) their quality amidst concerns over extremists recruitment.[^cite_note-587] [Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Pakistan) is divided into six main levels, including nursery, primary, [middle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_school), [matriculation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_school_\(upper_secondary\)), [intermediate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_college), and university programs.[^cite_note-edu2-585] Additionally, [private schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schools_in_Pakistan) offer a parallel secondary education system based on the curriculum set by the [Cambridge International Examinations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambridge_International_Examinations),[^cite_note-588] with 439 international schools reported in Pakistan.[^cite_note-589]&#x20;



[Malala Yousafzai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malala_Yousafzai) was awarded the [Nobel Peace Prize](26230922.md) in 2014, alongside [Kailash Satyarthi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kailash_Satyarthi) of India, for her advocacy of educational initiatives, particularly girls' education worldwide.

Initiatives since 2007 made [English medium education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_medium_education) mandatory nationwide. Following a 2012 attack on activist [Malala Yousafzai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malala_Yousafzai) by the [Taliban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehrik-i_Taliban_Pakistan), she became the youngest Nobel laureate for her education advocacy.[^cite_note-590] Reforms in 2013 mandated Chinese language courses in Sindh, reflecting China's growing influence. As of 2018, Pakistan's literacy rate stands at 62.3%, with significant regional and gender disparities.[^cite_note-591] Government initiatives, including computer literacy since 1995, aim to eradicate illiteracy, targeting 100% enrollment among primary school-age children and an \~86% literacy rate by 2015.[^cite_note-592] Pakistan allocates 2.3% of its GDP to education,[^cite_note-593] among the lowest in South Asia.[^cite_note-594]&#x20;

## Culture

Main articles: [Culture of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Pakistan), [British heritage of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_heritage_of_Pakistan), and [Public holidays in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_holidays_in_Pakistan)



Artwork by [Sadequain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadequain) on the ceiling of [Frere Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frere_Hall). Having painted around 15,000 paintings, Sadequain is considered one of the finest painters and calligraphers Pakistan has ever produced.

[Civil society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Pakistan) in Pakistan is hierarchical, emphasising [local cultural etiquette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Etiquette_in_Pakistan) and traditional Islamic values. The primary family unit is the [extended family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extended_family), but there's a rising trend towards [nuclear families](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_families) due to socio-economic factors.[^cite_note-nuclear-595][^cite_note-596] Both men and women typically wear *[Shalwar Kameez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shalwar_Kameez)*; men also favour trousers, jeans, and shirts.[^cite_note-taxila-597] The middle class has grown to about 35 million, with another 17 million in the upper and upper-middle classes, leading to a shift in power from rural landowners to urban elites.[^cite_note-598] Festivals like *[Eid ul-Fitr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eid_ul-Fitr)*, *[Eid ul-Azha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eid_ul-Azha)*, [Ramadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramadan), [Christmas](6237.md), [Easter](9325.md), [Holi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holi), and [Diwali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diwali) are primarily religious.[^cite_note-nuclear-595] Pakistan ranked 56th on the 2006 [A.T. Kearney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A.T._Kearney)/FP [Globalization Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalization_Index) due to increasing globalisation.[^cite_note-599]&#x20;

### Architecture

Main articles: [Pakistani architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_architecture) and [Hindu, Jain and Buddhist architectural heritage of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu,_Jain_and_Buddhist_architectural_heritage_of_Pakistan)



The [Lahore Fort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahore_Fort), a landmark built during the Mughal era, is a [UNESCO World Heritage Site](44940.md).

Four periods define Pakistani architecture: [pre-Islamic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Pakistan#Early_history), [Islamic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Pakistan#Muslim_period), [colonial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Pakistan#Colonial_era), and [post-colonial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Pakistan#Independence). The onset of the [Indus civilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_civilisation) around the mid-3rd millennium BCE heralded an urban culture, evidenced by surviving large structures.[^cite_note-600] Notable pre-Islamic settlements include [Mohenjo-daro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohenjo-daro), [Harappa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harappa), and [Kot Diji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kot_Diji).[^cite_note-mountains-601] The fusion of Buddhism and [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_campaign_of_Alexander_the_Great) influences birthed a distinctive [Greco-Buddhist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Buddhism) style from the 1st century CE, exemplified by the renowned [Gandhara style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gandara_art).[^cite_note-602] Notable [Buddhist architectural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_architecture) remnants include the [Takht-i-Bahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Takht-i-Bahi) monastery in [Khyber Pakhtunkhwa](21950.md).[^cite_note-603]&#x20;

The advent of Islam in present-day Pakistan marked the cessation of Buddhist architecture, ushering in [Islamic architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_architecture). The notable [Indo-Islamic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Islamic_architecture) structure, the [tomb of Shah Rukn-i-Alam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rukn-e-Alam) in Multan, remains significant. During the Mughal era, Persian-Islamic design merged with Hindustani art, seen in Lahore's architectural gems like the [Badshahi Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badshahi_Mosque) and the [Lahore Fort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahore_Fort) with the iconic [Alamgiri Gate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alamgiri_Gate). Lahore also boasts the vibrant [Wazir Khan Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wazir_Khan_Mosque),[^cite_note-604] and the lush [Shalimar Gardens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shalimar_Gardens_\(Lahore\)). In the British colonial period, Indo-European buildings emerged, blending European and Indian-Islamic styles. Post-colonial identity shines through modern landmarks like the [Faisal Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faisal_Mosque), [Minar-e-Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minar-e-Pakistan), and [Mazar-e-Quaid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazar-e-Quaid). [British architectural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_the_United_Kingdom) influence persists in structures across Lahore, Peshawar, and Karachi.[^cite_note-605]&#x20;

### Clothing, arts, and fashion

Main articles: [Pakistani clothing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_clothing), [Shalwar kameez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shalwar_kameez), [Sherwani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sherwani), [Jinnah cap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jinnah_cap), [Peshawari chappal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peshawari_chappal), [Pakol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakol), and [Sindhi topi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindhi_topi)



A depiction of traditional clothing of women from [Sindh](28972.md)

The *[Shalwar kameez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shalwar_kameez)* is Pakistan's [national dress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_dress), worn in all provinces: Punjab, Sindh, Balochistan, Khyber Pakhtunkhwa, and Azad Kashmir. Each province has its own style. Pakistanis wear a variety of fabrics like silk, [chiffon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiffon_\(fabric\)), and cotton. In addition to the national dress, men often wear domestically tailored suits and [neckties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Necktie), especially in offices, schools, and social gatherings.[^cite_note-606]&#x20;

Pakistan's [fashion industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_clothing) has thrived, blending traditional and modern styles to create a unique cultural identity. Regional and traditional dress remain significant symbols of native tradition, evolving into both modern and purer forms. Organisations like the Pakistan Fashion Design Council in [Lahore](125315.md) and the Fashion Pakistan Council in [Karachi](17123.md) host events like [PFDC Fashion Week](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PFDC_Sunsilk_Fashion_Week) and [Fashion Pakistan Week](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fashion_Pakistan_Week). Pakistan's inaugural fashion week took place in November 2009.[^cite_note-607]&#x20;

### Literature and philosophy

Main articles: [Literature of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literature_of_Pakistan), [Urdu poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu_poetry), and [Pakistani philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_philosophy)

Muhammad Iqbal

[Muhammad Iqbal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Iqbal), Pakistan's national poet who conceived the [idea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conception_of_Pakistan) of Pakistan

Pakistan boasts literature in various languages including [Urdu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu_literature), [Sindhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindhi_literature), [Punjabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_literature), [Pashto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashto_literature_and_poetry), [Baluchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balochi_Academy), [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_literature), [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_English_literature), and more.[^cite_note-608] The [Pakistan Academy of Letters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Academy_of_Letters) actively promotes literature and poetry both domestically and internationally.[^cite_note-609] [National Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Library_of_Pakistan) contributes to literary dissemination. Historically, Pakistani literature consisted mainly of [lyric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyric_poetry), [religious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufi_poetry), and [folkloric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_folklore) works, later diversifying under colonial influence into prose fiction, now widely embraced.[^cite_note-610][^cite_note-granta-611]&#x20;

The [national poet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_national_poets) of Pakistan, [Muhammad Iqbal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Iqbal), wrote influential poetry in Urdu and Persian, advocating for Islamic civilisational revival.[^cite_note-612] Notable figures in contemporary Urdu literature include [Josh Malihabadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josh_Malihabadi), [Faiz Ahmed Faiz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faiz_Ahmed_Faiz), and [Saadat Hasan Manto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saadat_Hasan_Manto).[^cite_note-granta-611] Popular Sufi poets like [Shah Abdul Latif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah_Abdul_Latif) and [Bulleh Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulleh_Shah) are revered.[^cite_note-613] [Mirza Kalich Beg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirza_Kalich_Beg) is hailed as the father of modern Sindhi prose.[^cite_note-614] Pakistani philosophy has been shaped by influences from British and American philosophy, with notable figures like [M. M. Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M._M._Sharif) contributing to its development.[^cite_note-615] After 1971, Marxist thought gained prominence in Pakistani philosophy through figures like [Jalaludin Abdur Rahim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jalaludin_Abdur_Rahim).[^cite_note-616]&#x20;

### Media and entertainment

Main articles: [Mass media in Pakistan](14937814.md), [Cinema of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Pakistan), [Music of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Pakistan), [History of Pakistani pop music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Pakistani_pop_music), [Theatre of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theatre_of_Pakistan), and [Pakistani dramas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_dramas)

The private [print media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_newspapers_in_Pakistan), state-owned [Pakistan Television Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Television_Corporation) (PTV), and [Pakistan Broadcasting Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Broadcasting_Corporation) (PBC) dominated media until the 21st century. Pakistan now boasts a vast network of domestic, privately owned [24-hour electronic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/24-hour_news_cycle) [news media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_television_channels_in_Pakistan) and [cable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cable_television) [television channels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_in_Pakistan).[^cite_note-IMSMedia2009-617]<sup>: 14–16, 21 </sup> The [Reporters Without Borders](526054.md) has indicated pressure faced by Pakistani reporters, particularly when reporting against the military or government.[^cite_note-RSF-618] The BBC describes Pakistani media as "among the most outspoken in South Asia".[^cite_note-619] Pakistani media has been instrumental in exposing corruption.[^cite_note-IMSMedia2009-617] In 2025, Pakistan ranked 158 out of 180 countries in the [Press Freedom Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Press_Freedom_Index) by [Reporters Without Borders](526054.md), highlighting restrictions on [freedom of the press](105323.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarès2025-620] According to the journalist [Raza Rumi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raza_Rumi), in 2023, TV channels in Pakistan faced suspensions and legal threats for airing content critical of the government or military, while online platforms also experienced temporary takedowns.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKumar2023-621] According to a 2025 report, some newspapers have faced financial pressure—such as withdrawal of government advertisements—for publishing content critical of government policies. Both military and civilian governments have historically used such tactics.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabolAli2025-622] [Freedom House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House) rated Pakistan's Internet freedom as "Not Free" in 2024.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEISP2026-436]&#x20;

The [Lollywood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lollywood), Punjabi, and [Pashto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashto_cinema) film industry is centered in Karachi, Lahore, and Peshawar. Although Bollywood films were banned from public cinemas from 1965 to 2008, they remained influential in Pakistani popular culture.[^cite_note-623] However, in 2019, the screening of Bollywood movies faced an indefinite ban.[^cite_note-624] Despite challenges faced by the Pakistani film industry, Urdu [televised dramas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_dramas) and theatrical performances remain popular, frequently broadcast by many entertainment media outlets.[^cite_note-625] Urdu dramas dominate the television entertainment industry, renowned for their quality since the 1990s.[^cite_note-626] Pakistani music encompasses diverse forms, from provincial folk music and traditional styles like [Qawwali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qawwali) and [Ghazal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazal) Gayaki to modern fusions of traditional and western music.[^cite_note-627] Pakistan boasts numerous renowned folk singers, and the arrival of Afghan refugees in western provinces has sparked interest in Pashto music, despite occasional intolerance.[^cite_note-628]&#x20;

### Cuisine

Main article: [Pakistani cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_cuisine)



[Roti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roti), served with various side dishes, is considered a staple food in Pakistan.

Pakistani cuisine, rooted in the royal kitchens of 16th-century Mughal emperors, blends influences from [British](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_cuisine), [Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_cuisine), [Central Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Asian_cuisine), and [Middle Eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Eastern_cuisine) culinary traditions.[^cite_note-629] Unlike Middle Eastern fare, Pakistani dishes are heavily spiced with garlic, ginger, turmeric, [chilli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chili_powder), and [garam masala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garam_masala). [Roti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roti), a wheat-based flatbread, accompanies most meals, alongside [curry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curry), meat, vegetables, and lentils. Rice is also common, served plain, spiced, or in sweet dishes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMohiuddin20073,_317,_323–324-183][^cite_note-630] [Lassi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lassi), a traditional drink from the [Punjab region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab_region), and [black tea with milk and sugar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_tea_culture) are popular beverages enjoyed nationwide.[^cite_note-taxila-597][^cite_note-631] [Sohan halwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sohan_halwa), a beloved sweet dish from southern Punjab, is savoured across Pakistan.[^cite_note-632]&#x20;

### Sports

Main article: [Sport in Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Pakistan)



A cricket match between Pakistan and Australia at [Lord's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord%27s)



The [Pakistan national hockey team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_men%27s_national_field_hockey_team) with the [1971 Hockey World Cup trophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1971_Men%27s_Hockey_World_Cup)

[Cricket](25675557.md) is the most popular sport in Pakistan, followed by [football](10568.md). [Field hockey](10886.md) is the national sport. Other sports like [squash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Squash_\(sport\)), [polo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polo), and [traditional games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_games_of_Pakistan) are also enjoyed.&#x20;

In cricket, Pakistan holds victories in all major [ICC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Cricket_Council) tournaments, including the [ICC Cricket World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ICC_Cricket_World_Cup), [ICC World Twenty20](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ICC_World_Twenty20), and [ICC Champions Trophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ICC_Champions_Trophy). The [Pakistan Super League](38199013.md) ranks among the top [T20 leagues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_T20_cricket_competitions) globally.[^cite_note-633][^cite_note-634]&#x20;

In football, Pakistan established the [Pakistan Football Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Football_Federation) soon after its creation, and it is known for producing [FIFA World Cup](11370.md) balls.[^cite_note-635][^cite_note-636]&#x20;

In field hockey, Pakistan holds four [Hockey World Cup](951198.md) wins, eight [Asian Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Field_hockey_at_the_Asian_Games) gold medals, and three [Olympic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Field_hockey_at_the_Summer_Olympics) gold medals. Squash player [Jahangir Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jahangir_Khan) holds the record for the longest winning streak in professional sport history, winning 555 consecutive matches.[^cite_note-637][^cite_note-638] Pakistan has hosted various international events, including Cricket and Hockey World Cups and Asian Games.[^cite_note-ICC-2021-639]&#x20;

[^cite_note-Rigveda-45]: &#x20;The precise time span of the period is uncertain. [Philological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philology) and [linguistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_linguistics) evidence indicates that the [Rigveda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rigveda), the oldest of the Vedas, was composed roughly between 1700 and 1100 BCE, also referred to as the early Vedic period. Oberlies gives an estimate of 1200–1100 BCE for the youngest hymns in book ten. Estimates for a *terminus post quem* of the earliest hymns are more uncertain. Other 'cumulative evidence' sets a wide range of 1700–1100.

[^cite_note-Wright-2009-14]: &#x20;[Wright (2009)](#CITEREFWright2009) "The Indus civilisation is one of three in the 'Ancient East' that, along with Mesopotamia and Pharaonic Egypt, was a cradle of early civilisation in the Old World (Childe, 1950). Mesopotamia and Egypt were longer lived, but coexisted with Indus civilisation during its florescence between 2600 and 1900 B.C. Of the three, the Indus was the most expansive, extending from today's northeast Afghanistan to Pakistan and India."

[^cite_note-Copland-2001-18]: &#x20;[Copland (2001)](#CITEREFCopland2001) "However, the real turning point for the new Muslim League came with the general election of December 1945 and January 1946. Despite facing a rejuvenated Congress, the League won four-fifths of all the Muslim-reserved seats ... The result left no one, not least the British, in doubt about where the locus of power within the Muslim community now lay ... In most respects, therefore, the League's success in the elections of 1945–46 can be interpreted as a clear Muslim mandate for Pakistan. (p 72)"

[^cite_note-Metcalf-2006-19]: &#x20;[Metcalf & Metcalf (2006)](#CITEREFMetcalfMetcalf2006) "The loss of life was immense, with estimates ranging from several hundred thousand up to a million. But, even for those who survived, fear generated a widespread perception that one could be safe only among members of one's own community; and this in turn helped consolidate loyalties towards the state, whether India or Pakistan, in which one might find a secure haven. This was especially important for Pakistan, where the succour it offered to Muslims gave that state for the first time a visible territorial reality. Fear too drove forward a mass migration unparalleled in the history of South Asia. ... Overall, partition uprooted some 12.5 million of undivided India's people."

[^cite_note-Coningham-Young-2015-36]: &#x20;[Coningham & Young (2015)](#CITEREFConinghamYoung2015) "Mehrgarh remains one of the key sites in South Asia because it has provided the earliest known undisputed evidence for farming and pastoral communities in the region, and its plant and animal material provide clear evidence for the ongoing manipulation, and domestication, of certain species. Perhaps most importantly in a South Asian context, the role played by zebu makes this a distinctive, localised development, with a character completely different from other parts of the world. Finally, the longevity of the site, and its articulation with the neighbouring site of Nausharo (c. 2800—2000 BCE), provides a very clear continuity from South Asia's first farming villages to the emergence of its first cities (Jarrige, 1984)."

[^cite_note-Fisher-2018-37]: &#x20;[Fisher (2018)](#CITEREFFisher2018) "The earliest discovered instance in India of well-established, settled agricultural society is at Mehrgarh in the hills between the Bolan Pass and the Indus plain (today in Pakistan) (see Map 3.1). From as early as 7000 BCE, communities there started investing increased labor in preparing the land and selecting, planting, tending, and harvesting particular grain-producing plants. They also domesticated animals, including sheep, goats, pigs, and oxen (both humped zebu \[Bos indicus] and unhumped \[Bos taurus]). Castrating oxen, for instance, turned them from mainly meat sources into domesticated draft-animals as well."

[^cite_note-Dyson-2018-38]: &#x20;[Dyson (2018)](#CITEREFDyson2018) "The subcontinent's people were hunter-gatherers for many millennia. There were very few of them. Indeed, 10,000 years ago there may only have been a couple of hundred thousand people, living in small, often isolated groups, the descendants of various 'modern' human incomers. Then, perhaps linked to events in Mesopotamia, about 8,500 years ago agriculture emerged in Baluchistan."

[^cite_note-Allchin-1982-39]: &#x20;[Allchin & Allchin (1982)](#CITEREFAllchinAllchin1982) "During the second half of the fourth and early part of the third millennium B.C., a new development begins to become apparent in the greater Indus system, which we can now see to be a formative stage underlying the Mature Indus of the middle and late third millennium. This development seems to have involved the whole Indus system, and to a lesser extent the Indo-Iranian borderlands to its west, but largely left untouched the subcontinent east of the Indus system."

[^cite_note-Sengupta-2023-78]: &#x20;[Sengupta (2023)](#CITEREFSengupta2023) "Syed Ahmad Khan, the founder of the Muhammadan Anglo-Oriental College (which later became Aligarh Muslim University), had declared in a speech in Meerut what would become famous as the "two nation theory.""......"Is it possible that under these circumstances two nations — the Mahomedans and the Hindus — could sit on the same throne and remain equal in power? Most certainly not."

[^cite_note-Hardy-1972-94]: &#x20;[Hardy (1972)](#CITEREFHardy1972) "Much has been made' of the failure of Congress and the Muslim parties to agree over the Nehru Report and of the rejection of Jinnah's 'Fourteen Points' as a significant milestone along the way to the partition of India. A great opportunity was lost, it is thought, for the abandonment of separate electorates by voluntary Muslim agreement."

[^cite_note-Wuthnow-2013-95]: &#x20;[Wuthnow (2013)](#CITEREFWuthnow2013) "To satisfy Muslims' determination to have guaranteed rights in the future political system of India and to maintain territorial unity of the Indian state, by 1929 Jinnah produced the formula known as the Fourteen Points of Mr. Jinnah. The Fourteen Points included separate electorates for Muslims in the provinces of India, parity of electoral representation in the Punjab and Bengal, and electoral considerations for Muslims in those provinces in which they were a minority, although they would retain clear majority in the Northwest Frontier Province, Baluchistan, and Sind."

[^cite_note-Singh-Shani-2021-96]: &#x20;[Singh & Shani (2021)](#CITEREFSinghShani2021) "Jinnah's famous 'fourteen points' as a condition for support for India's unity, with strong provinces within a weak Indian federation, marked the parting of ways between the Congress and the Muslim national leadership (Jalal 1994, 10–11). At the 1930 session of the All-Indian Muslim Conference, Sir Mohammed Iqbal proposed a Muslim homeland that would serve 'as a symbolic cultural expression of the common striving of Muslim fulfilment – a political manifestation of a common mission' (Gilmartin 1988, 167). The idea of self-determination for India's Muslims was constructed mainly in fear of the majoritarian 'secular' (Hindu) nationalism of the Congress."

[^cite_note-Iqbal-97]: &#x20;Iqbal (two-nation theory): multiple sources: [N. Khan (2012)](#CITEREFN._Khan2012) [Basu & Miroshnik (2023)](#CITEREFBasuMiroshnik2023) "Mohammed Iqbal was credited with coming up with the two-nation theory in his speech at Allahabad in 1930 to the Muslim League in a very formal way by saying: "I would like to see the Punjab, North-West Frontier Province, Sind, and Baluchistan amalgamated into a single State. Self-government within the British Empire or without the British Empire, the formation of a consolidated North-West Indian Moslem State appears to me to be the final destiny of the Moslems, at least of NorthWest India" (Zaidi, 1993; Ahmed, 1970)." [Hussain (2018)](#CITEREFHussain2018) "After repeated demands for stronger constitutional safeguards to protect the rights of minorities, Iqbal eventually opted for a separate Islamic Republic instead. As opposed to putting the free and rational individual at the centre of his democratic theory, Iqbal's republic primarily required Muslims endowed with a specific character and smelted together by a peculiar vision of individuality. Like a number of his contemporaries, Iqbal warmed up to the two nation-theory. Unlike the mainstream view, however, which read an eternal struggle of Hindus and Muslims back into Indian history, Iqbal's concept of the Muslim nation was something to be striven towards, not something to be replanted from the past. Iqbal believed that the best way to actualize this national sentiment in the present, was through individual political action."

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPandeya2003-98]: &#x20;[Pandeya 2003](#CITEREFPandeya2003).

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBasuMiroshnik2017-99]: &#x20;[Basu & Miroshnik 2017](#CITEREFBasuMiroshnik2017).

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEM._H._Khan2016-100]: &#x20;[M. H. Khan 2016](#CITEREFM._H._Khan2016).

[^cite_note-Tucker-2020-101]: &#x20;[Tucker (2020)](#CITEREFTucker2020) "Gandhi's decision played directly into the hands of Jinnah. Jinnah's Muslim League strongly supported the Allied war effort and thereby greatly advanced the possibility of the creation of a separate Muslim state in the Indian subcontinent after the war."

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChandra2008-102]: &#x20;[Chandra 2008](#CITEREFChandra2008).

[^cite_note-Mohiuddin-2007-1-103]: &#x20;[Mohiuddin (2007](#CITEREFMohiuddin2007), p. 70) "In the elections of 1946, the Muslim League won 90 percent of the legislative seats reserved for Muslims. It was the power of the big zamindars in Punjab and Sindh behind the Muslim League candidates that led to this massive landslide victory (Alavi 2002, 14). Even Congress, which had always denied the League's claim to be the only true representative of Indian Muslims had to concede the truth of that claim. The 1946 election was, in effect, a plebiscite among Muslims on Pakistan."

[^cite_note-Mohiuddin-2007-2-104]: &#x20;[Mohiuddin (2007](#CITEREFMohiuddin2007), p. 71) "Despite the League's victory in the elections, the British did not want the partition of British India. As a last attempt to avoid it, Britain put forward the Cabinet Mission Plan, according to which India would become a federation of three large, self-governing provinces and the central government would be limited to power over foreign policy and defense, implying a weak center."

[^cite_note-Riots-1-111]: &#x20;Casualties/Genocide: multiple sources: [Sikand (2004)](#CITEREFSikand2004) [Butalia (2000)](#CITEREFButalia2000) [Isaacs (1975)](#CITEREFIsaacs1975) "2,000,000 killed in the Hindu-Muslim holocaust during the partition of British-India and the creation of India and Pakistan" [Basrur (2008)](#CITEREFBasrur2008) "An estimated 12–15 million people were displaced, and some 2 million died. The legacy of Partition (never without a capital P) remains strong today ..." [D'Costa (2011)](#CITEREFD'Costa2011) "Estimates of the dead vary from 200,000 (the contemporary British figure) to 2 million (a subsequent Indian speculation). Today, however, it is widely accepted that nearly a million people died during Partition (Butalia, 1997)." [Brass (2003)](#CITEREFBrass2003) "In the event, largely but not exclusively as a consequence of their efforts, the entire Muslim population of the eastern Punjab districts migrated to West Punjab and the entire Sikh and Hindu populations moved to East Punjab in the midst of widespread intimidation, terror, violence, abduction, rape, and murder."

[^cite_note-Riots-2-112]: &#x20;Rape figures: multiple sources: [Visweswaran (2011)](#CITEREFVisweswaran2011) [Daiya (2011)](#CITEREFDaiya2011) "The official estimate of the number of abducted women during Partition was placed at 33,000 non-Muslim (Hindu or Sikh predominantly) women in Pakistan, and 50,000 Muslim women in India." [Abraham (2002)](#CITEREFAbraham2002) "In addition thousands of women on both sides of the newly formed borders (estimated range from 29,000 to 50,000 Muslim women and 15,000 to 35,000 Hindu and Sikh women) were abducted, raped, forced to convert, forced into marriage, forced back into what the two States defined as 'their proper homes', torn apart from their families once during partition by those who abducted them, and again, after partition, by the State which tried to 'recover' and 'rehabilitate' them." [Singh, Iyer & Gairola (2016)](#CITEREFSinghIyerGairola2016) "The horrific statistics that surround women refugees-between 75,000–100,000 Hindu, Muslim and Sikh women who were abducted by men of the other communities, subjected to multiple rapes, mutilations, and, for some, forced marriages and conversions-is matched by the treatment of the abducted women in the hands of the nation-state. In the Constituent Assembly in 1949 it was recorded that of the 50,000 Muslim women abducted in India, 8,000 of then were recovered, and of the 33,000 Hindu and Sikh women abducted, 12,000 were recovered."

[^cite_note-Hasan-Raza-2009-113]: &#x20;[Hasan & Raza (2009](#CITEREFHasanRaza2009), p. 12) "When the British Indian Empire was partitioned in 1947, 4.7 million Sikhs and Hindus left what is today Pakistan for India, and 6.5 million Muslims migrated from India to Pakistan."

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERiggs2024-114]: &#x20;[Riggs 2024](#CITEREFRiggs2024).

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBhaumik1996-115]: &#x20;[Bhaumik 1996](#CITEREFBhaumik1996).

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKazmi2003-116]: &#x20;[Kazmi 2003](#CITEREFKazmi2003).

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETucker2017-117]: &#x20;[Tucker 2017](#CITEREFTucker2017).

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAkbar2018-118]: &#x20;[Akbar 2018](#CITEREFAkbar2018).

[^cite_note-Kumarasingham-2013-119]: &#x20;[Kumarasingham (2013)](#CITEREFKumarasingham2013) "Few today, including those who work on the subcontinent, recollect that India, Pakistan and Sri Lanka did not become republics the day British rule ended. Even distinguished scholars of Empire like Perry Anderson and A. G. Hopkins have made the common assumption that India naturally became a republic upon independence on 15 August 1947. Instead, all three of these South Asian states began their independent life as Realms within the British Commonwealth and mirrored the style and institutions of the Dominions of Canada, Australia, South Africa and New Zealand. Though their sovereignty was in no way impaired by this seemingly ambiguous position they all held the British sovereign as their head of state who was represented in each capital by a governor- general appointed on the advice of the local prime minister. India, Pakistan and Ceylon were Realms from 1947 to 1950, 1947 to 1956 and 1948 to 1972 respectively."

[^cite_note-McGrath-1996-120]: &#x20;[McGrath (1996)](#CITEREFMcGrath1996) "Undivided India, their magnificent imperial trophy, was besmirched by the creation of Pakistan, and the division of India was never emotionally accepted by many British leaders, Mountbatten among them."

[^cite_note-Ahmed-1997-121]: &#x20;[Ahmed (1997)](#CITEREFAhmed1997) "Mountbatten's partiality was apparent in his own statements. He tilted openly and heavily towards Congress. While doing so he clearly expressed his lack of support and faith in the Muslim League and its Pakistan idea."

[^cite_note-Wolpert-2009-122]: &#x20;[Wolpert (2009)](#CITEREFWolpert2009) "Mountbatten tried to convince Jinnah of the value of accepting him, Mountbatten, as Pakistan's first governor-general, but Jinnah refused to be moved from his determination to take that job himself."

[^cite_note-Lapierre-Collins-2015-123]: &#x20;[Lapierre & Collins (2015)](#CITEREFLapierreCollins2015) "Not only was I not aware, but nobody was aware. Nobody had a clue. I'm glad I didn't because I just don't know what I would have done if I'd known that. You see, Jinnah was so much of a one-man band. If somebody had told me he's going to be dead in x months would I then -I am asking myself this question now-would I have said, Let's hold India together and not divide it? Would I have put back the clock, and held the position? Most probably. I have a feeling Jinnah may not have known himself he had tuberculosis. He was a very severe, cold and repressed person. Nothing would have surprised me about him. He was an extraordinary creature."

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson2009-124]: &#x20;[Wilson 2009](#CITEREFWilson2009).

[^cite_note-Hussain-2008-1-125]: &#x20;[Hussain (2008)](#CITEREFHussain2008) "Mawlānā Shabbīr Ahmad Usmānī, a respected Deobandī ʿālim (scholar) who was appointed to the prestigious position of Shaykh al-Islām of Pakistan in 1949, was the first to demand that Pakistan become an Islamic state. But Mawdūdī and his Jamāʿat-i Islāmī played the central part in the demand for an Islamic constitution. Mawdūdī demanded that the Constituent Assembly make an unequivocal declaration affirming the "supreme sovereignty of God" and the supremacy of the sharīʿah as the basic law of Pakistan."

[^cite_note-Hussain-2008-2-126]: &#x20;[Hussain (2008)](#CITEREFHussain2008) "The first important result of the combined efforts of the Jamāʿat-i Islāmī and the ʿulamāʿ was the passage of the Objectives Resolution in March 1949, whose formulation reflected compromise between traditionalists and modernists. The resolution embodied "the main principles on which the constitution of Pakistan is to be based". It declared that "sovereignty over the entire universe belongs to God Almighty alone and the authority which He has delegated to the State of Pakistan through its people for being exercised within the limits prescribed by Him is a sacred trust", that "the principles of democracy, freedom, equality, tolerance and social justice, as enunciated by Islam shall be fully observed", and that "the Muslims shall be enabled to order their lives in the individual and collective spheres in accord with the teaching and requirements of Islam as set out in the Holy Qurʿan and Sunna". The Objectives Resolution has been reproduced as a preamble to the constitutions of 1956, 1962, and 1973."

[^cite_note-Dhulipala-2015-1-258]: &#x20;[Dhulipala (2015](#CITEREFDhulipala2015), p. 496) "The idea of Pakistan may have had its share of ambiguities, but its dismissal as a vague emotive symbol hardly illuminates the reasons as to why it received such overwhelmingly popular support among Indian Muslims, especially those in the 'minority provinces' of British India such as U.P."

[^cite_note-Dhulipala-2015-2-259]: &#x20;[Dhulipala (2015](#CITEREFDhulipala2015), p. 497) "As the book has demonstrated, local ML functionaries, (U.P.) ML leadership, Muslim modernists at Aligarh, the ulama and even Jinnah at times articulated their vision of Pakistan in terms of an Islamic state."

[^cite_note-Dhulipala-2015-3-260]: &#x20;[Dhulipala (2015](#CITEREFDhulipala2015), p. 489) "But what is undeniable is the close association he developed with the ulama, for when he died a little over a year after Pakistan was born, Maulana Shabbir Ahmad Usmani, in his funeral oration, described Jinnah as the greatest Muslim after the Mughal Emperor Aurangzeb."……"Similarly, Usmani asked Pakistanis to remember the Qaid's ceaseless message of Unity, Faith and Discipline and work to fulfil his dream to create a solid bloc of all Muslim states from Karachi to Ankara, from Pakistan to Morocco. He \[Jinnah] wanted to see the Muslims of the world united under the banner of Islam as an effective check against the aggressive designs of their enemies."

[^cite_note-Haqqani-2010-1-261]: &#x20;[Haqqani (2010](#CITEREFHaqqani2010), p. 16) "The first formal step toward transforming Pakistan into an Islamic ideological state was taken in March 1949 when the country's first prime minister, Liaquat Ali Khan, presented the Objectives Resolution in the constituent assembly."

[^cite_note-Dhulipala-2015-4-262]: &#x20;[Dhulipala (2015](#CITEREFDhulipala2015), p. 491) "Khaliq drew a sharp distinction between this Islamic state and a Muslim state. He claimed that as of now Pakistan was only a Muslim state in view of the majority of its population being Muslim, and indeed could never be an Islamic state by itself. It could certainly fulfill its promise and destiny by bringing together all the believers of Islam into one political unit and it is only then that an Islamic state would be achieved."

[^cite_note-Haqqani-2010-2-263]: &#x20;[Haqqani (2010](#CITEREFHaqqani2010), p. 18) "One of the earliest Western scholars of Pakistani politics, Keith Callard, observed that Pakistanis seemed to believe in the essential unity of purpose and outlook in the Muslim world: Pakistan was founded to advance the cause of Muslims. Other Muslims might have been expected to be sympathetic, even enthusiastic. But this assumed that other Muslim states would take the same view of the relation between religion and nationality."

[^cite_note-Haqqani-2010-3-264]: &#x20;[Haqqani (2010](#CITEREFHaqqani2010), p. 18) "Pakistan's pan-Islamic aspirations, however, were neither shared nor supported by the Muslim governments of the time. Nationalism in other parts of the Muslim world was based on ethnicity, language, or territory."

[^cite_note-Haqqani-2010-4-265]: &#x20;[Haqqani (2010](#CITEREFHaqqani2010), p. 19) "Although Muslim governments were initially unsympathetic to Pakistan's pan-Islamic aspirations, Islamists from the world over were drawn to Pakistan. Controversial figures such as the pro-Nazi former grand mufti of Palestine, Al-Haj Amin al-Husseini, and leaders of Islamist political movements like the Arab Muslim Brotherhood became frequent visitors to the country."

[^cite_note-Pasha-2005-1-330]: &#x20;[Pasha (2005](#CITEREFPasha2005), p. 225) "Pakistan's expression of solidarity was followed, after Independence, by a vigorous pursuit of bilateral relations with Muslim countries like Iran and Turkey."

[^cite_note-Pasha-2005-2-331]: &#x20;[Pasha (2005](#CITEREFPasha2005), p. 226) "Following Khaliquzzaman, the Ali brothers had sought to project Pakistan, with its comparatively larger manpower and military strength, as the natural leader of the Islamic world."

[^cite_note-Dhulipala-2015-5-332]: &#x20;[Dhulipala (2015](#CITEREFDhulipala2015), p. 18) "As a top ranking ML leader Khaliquzzaman declared, 'Pakistan would bring all Muslim countries together into Islamistan – a pan-Islamic entity'."

[^cite_note-Haqqani-2013-1-333]: &#x20;[Haqqani (2013](#CITEREFHaqqani2013), pp. 20–21) "Within a few years the president of the Muslim League, Chaudhry Khaliq-uz-Zaman, announced that Pakistan would bring all Muslim countries together into Islamistan – a pan-Islamic entity. None of these developments within the new country elicited approval among Americans for the idea of India's partition ... British Prime Minister Clement Attlee voiced the international consensus at the time when he told the House of Commons of his hope that 'this severance may not endure.' He hoped that the proposed dominions of India and Pakistan would in course of time, come together to form one great Member State of the British Commonwealth of Nations."

[^cite_note-Haqqani-2013-2-334]: &#x20;[Haqqani (2013](#CITEREFHaqqani2013), p. 22) "During this time most of the Arab world was going through a nationalist awakening. Pan-Islamic dreams involving the unification of Muslim countries, possibly under Pakistani leadership, had little attraction."

[^cite_note-Roberts-2003-335]: &#x20;[Roberts (2003)](#CITEREFRoberts2003) "The following year, Choudhry Khaliquzzaman toured the Middle East, pleading for the formation of an alliance or confederation of Muslim states. The Arab states, often citing Pakistan's inability to solve its problems with Muslim neighbor Afghanistan, showed little enthusiasm ... Some saw the effort to form 'Islamistan' as a Pakistani attempt to dominate other Muslim states."

[^cite_note-Pande-2011-2-339]: &#x20;[Pande (2011](#CITEREFPande2011), p. 159) "Both Saudi Arabia and Iran used Pakistan as a battleground for their proxy war for the 'hearts and minds' of Pakistani Sunnis and Shias with the resultant rise in sectarian tensions in Pakistan. The rise of the Taliban in Afghanistan in the 1990s further strained Pakistan-Iran relations. Pakistan's support of the Sunni Pashtun organization created problems for Shia Iran for whom a Taliban-controlled Afghanistan was a nightmare."

[^cite_note-Pande-2011-1-348]: &#x20;[Pande (2011](#CITEREFPande2011), p. 178) "The belief that the creation of Pakistan made Pakistan the true leader of Muslim causes around the world led Pakistan's diplomats to vigorously champion the cause of self-determination for fellow Muslims at the United Nations. Pakistan's founders, including Jinnah, supported anti-colonial movements: "Our heart and soul go out in sympathy with those who are struggling for their freedom ... If subjugation and exploitation are carried on, there will be no peace and there will be no end to wars." [^cite_note-Middle_power_nation-363]: &#x20;Middle power: multiple sources: [Buzan (2004)](#CITEREFBuzan2004) [Solomon (1997)](#CITEREFSolomon1997) [Rajagopalan (2011)](#CITEREFRajagopalan2011) [Buzan & Waever (2003)](#CITEREFBuzanWaever2003) [Paul (2012](#CITEREFPaul2012), p. 11) "The regional powers such as Israel or Pakistan are not simple bystanders of great power politics in their regions; they attempt to asymmetrically influence the major power system often in their own distinct ways." [Vandamme (2014](#CITEREFVandamme2014), p. 14) "Countries like Saudi Arabia and Pakistan have enough influence to not be considered small, but not enough to be major powers. Within the limits of their regions, they play a significant political role. Thus instinctively, they would qualify as middle powers. While it is not the objective here to question the characteristics of Jordan's definition of middle powers, we argue that Pakistan is in fact a middle power despite its being nuclear-armed. When looking at the numbers, for instance, it appears that Saudi Arabia and Pakistan can be classified as middle powers."
